## H I G H L I G H T S                                      G R A P H I C A L A B S T R A C T

• Review of the components, architecture and assumptions of urban microclimate models • Methodologies and numerical models to handle      street-scale   microclimatic variables • Model validation issues and solutions for determining physical processes • Recommendations to provide reliable values for thermal comfort indices • The role of irrigation in enhancing the cooling effects drawn from urban vegetation [SOURCE: Page_1]

## A R T I C L E I N F O                                    A B S T R A C T

Keywords:                                                The repeated occurrence, intensity and frequency of urban heatwaves pose significant challenges to urban areas. Irrigation                                               This paper critically reviews the current state of modelling the impact of irrigated vegetation on urban micro­ Microclimate models                                      climate. It synthesizes findings from recent publications on the subject, focusing on the methodologies, chal­ Urban thermal comfort lenges, and advances in numerical models that describe thermal comfort and heat mitigation strategies. Street canyon Numerical model Emphasizing the need for a transdisciplinary approach, the review highlights the key thermal and water-related factors influencing urban microclimates, including aerodynamic, radiative and phase change (e.g. evapotrans­ piration) processes, in interplay with urban geometry, materials and vegetation, especially when irrigated on purpose. The paper categorizes existing models based on their applications, physical modelling capabilities, and spatial-temporal discretization, offering guidelines for model selection tailored to specific urban planning ob­ jectives. Additionally, it provides a detailed description of the most pertinent models, especially regarding vegetation modelling for urban areas. The review underscores the necessity of validating existing models and the [SOURCE: Page_1]

Fig. 2. (a) Search results on the Web of Science: 517 articles found, of which 39 % (217 articles) were out of scope, 1 % (8 articles) were either unavailable or behind paywalls, 4 % (19 articles) were literature review articles, and 56 % (273 articles) contained information regarding microclimate models that describe the impact of vegetation. (b) The 273 articles dealing with microclimate models that describe the impact of vegetation mentioned 22 numerical models, whose names and number of citations appear in the bar graph. Fig. 4. Summary of general information (including model types, see Fig. 3), Physical Modelling, Applications and Outputs of the 22 numerical models identified in this literature review. Cells are ticked with labels “Yes” (widely applied in literature/practice), “Possible” (theoretically feasible but with few or no examples in the literature), or “No” (not provided by the software). Thermal comfort variables are air temperature (Ta), relative humidity (RH), mean radiant temperature (Tmrt), wind speed, Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI), Physiological Equivalent Temperature (PET) and others. Models marked with (*) are detailed in the next sections. Table adapted from Muanda (2024). [SOURCE: Page_7]

Fig. 5. Simplified framework for the validation of numerical models. [SOURCE: Page_12]

addressed, as such combinations seem promising in acting upon all the constitutive variables of thermal comfort indices. Moreover, diverse growth and irrigation strategies should be considered, exploring their ability to enhance evapotranspiration fluxes (Fig. 7) and even the op­ portunity of different irrigation piloting strategies during heatwaves or standard periods (Garcia De Cezar et al., 2024; Kubilay et al., 2023). In a wider perspective, studying plant combinations should also lead to studying different arrangements and vegetation layouts, at different connected and embedded scales, so as to think the issue in terms of green (and blue) grids, in certain streets and across the city (Fu et al., 2022). Fig. 6. Fig. 8. Diagram showing the clustering of the 22 numerical models identified in this study. The clusters are classified based on their functionalities: direct or indirect calculation of thermal comfort, CFD or coupled models and others, complex vegetation representation (multiple effects of vegetation on the microclimate) or simple representation, and the number of citations of the models in each cluster. Additionally, the models are classified according to their typical usage purpose, complexity, and spatial discretization. [SOURCE: Page_14]
